1	# Cycle protocol
2	
3	This protocol uses [Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It
4	contains instructions on how a commit cycle is accomplished.
5	
6	The artifact a cycle produces is whatever the bot generates from
7	the conversation — code, prose, an image, a song, a screenplay.
8	The steps below use a Rust crate as the running example (the
9	cargo cycle, `Cargo.toml` versioning); substitute your medium's
10	equivalents — this project's manifest is recorded in
11	[versioning.md](versioning.md).
12	
13	## Cycles
14	
15	A cycle has three phases:
16	
17	- **[Preparation](#preparation)** (`X.Y.Z-0`) — the cycle's
18	  first commit, when it needs setup (a lightweight cycle omits
19	  it and starts at `-1` — see
20	  [versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)). Sets up the
21	  cycle:
22	  - Bump the version-of-record to `X.Y.Z-0` (where it lives
23	    and the suffix scheme are project-specific — see
24	    [versioning.md](versioning.md)).
25	  - Pick up a `## Todo` item (typically the top-ranked,
26	    #1) into `## In Progress` (bold title + succinct problem
27	    statement + plan ladder).
28	  - Open the [chores section](#chores-sections).
29	- **[Work-N](#work-n)** (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …) — the
30	  commits that implement the change. As many as the change
31	  needs; each runs through the
32	  [per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow).
33	- **[Close-out](#close-out)** (bare `X.Y.Z`) — the cycle's
34	  last commit. Bookkeeping only:
35	  - Move the picked-up item to a one-line entry in
36	    `## Done`.
37	  - Move the `## In Progress` block into the
38	    [chores section](#chores-sections).
39	  - Optionally update `notes/README.md` if functionality
40	    changed.
41	
42	A cycle's commits are published to the project remote
43	either incrementally or as one batch at close-out; the
44	result must always be published at close-out. See
45	[Pushing](#pushing).
46	
47	**Sub-cycles.** When a Work commit's scope grows enough to
48	warrant its own ladder, it subdivides — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
49	Preparation, `X.Y.Z-3.1` / `X.Y.Z-3.2` Work, `X.Y.Z-3`
50	Close-out. The same three-phase shape applies recursively
51	at every depth. See [Numbering](#numbering) for the
52	suffix rule and [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)
53	for the local-ladder mechanics.
54	
55	## Chores sections
56	
57	A **chores section** is a `##` section in
58	`notes/chores/chores-NN.md` recording landed work. In
59	general, every commit that lands on the permanent branch
60	should have a reference to it on a `Commits:` list in a
61	chores file.
62	
63	The phrase **"Open" the chores section** means append a
64	`##` header to the current `notes/chores/chores-NN.md`
65	with the title it records (e.g. `## refactor: foo bar`),
66	followed by an **empty `Commits:`** line. The narrative
67	body stays empty until close-out (see [Close-out](#close-out));
68	the `Commits:` line is backfilled later, once the commit is
69	permanent (see [Commits backfill](#commits-backfill) below).
70	
71	Fuller chores conventions (content rules, header sync,
72	design subsection pattern, `Commits:` formatting) live in
73	AGENTS.md [Chores conventions](../AGENTS.md#chores-conventions).
74	
75	### Commits backfill
76	
77	A chores section's `Commits:` line cites the commit(s) it
78	records, by SHA — but a SHA isn't stable until the commit
79	lands on a **permanent branch** (`main`, or a long-lived
80	release/patch branch that won't be rewritten); a rebase or
81	squash rewrites it on the way. So:
82	
83	- A chores section is **opened with an empty `Commits:`** line.
84	- **Backfill once the commit is on a permanent branch**,
85	  where its SHA is final. A commit can't record its own SHA
86	  (that would change the hash), so the fill always lands one
87	  push later: **each push backfills the `Commits:` of the
88	  commits the previous push made permanent.** On a topic
89	  branch the sections instead wait until the branch lands —
90	  so no SHA is ever written that a later rebase could
91	  invalidate.
92	
93	Use `[[N]]` refs — several as `[[N]],[[M]]` only when one
94	section records multiple commits (a merge non-ff close-out) —
95	with the commit URL + 40-hex SHA in the file's `# References`
96	(format in AGENTS.md
97	[Chores commit references](../AGENTS.md#chores-commit-references)).
98	A section's `##` title matches its commit title, so a rare
99	deliberate rewrite of a permanent-branch commit re-syncs via
100	`git log --grep "<title>"`.
101	
102	The per-push cadence is a project choice, not dogma — a
103	**per-close-out** model (recording a cycle's SHAs at its
104	close-out) is equally valid. The one invariant: a recorded
105	SHA must be permanent.
106	
107	## Preparation
108	
109	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`), when the cycle needs
110	setup (a lightweight cycle omits it — see
111	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)):
112	
113	- **Bump the version-of-record** to `X.Y.Z-0`. Where it
114	  lives, the suffix scheme, and any derived files (a
115	  lockfile, a sourced manifest version) are
116	  project-specific — see [versioning.md](versioning.md).
117	- **Move a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one) into
118	  `## In Progress` and the todo item should have:
119	  - A **bold title line** — that will be the chores
120	    section header, minus the `## ` prefix.
121	  - A **succinct problem statement**; add if one is needed
122	  - A **plan ladder**.
123	- **Open the [chores section](#chores-sections)** —
124	  append a `##` header with the title it records — the
125	  cycle's anticipated close-out title.
126	
127	## Work-N
128	
129	The cycle's work commits (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …)
130	implement the change. As many as needed:
131	
132	- Each commit runs through the
133	  **[per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow)**.
134	- **Interim pushes** are optional (backup, progress
135	  visibility).
136	- Close-out is the only mandatory push (see
137	  [Pushing](#pushing)).
138	- **Subdivide into a sub-cycle** if a Work commit's
139	  scope grows enough (see
140	  [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)).
141	
142	## Close-out
143	
144	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) does bookkeeping
145	only — the commit body describes that bookkeeping, not
146	what happens post-squash:
147	
148	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
149	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
150	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the
151	  [chores section](#chores-sections)** (the title-only
152	  placeholder opened during Preparation):
153	  - Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
154	    `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the
155	    chores `##` header already carries that string).
156	  - Paste under the chores `##` header.
157	  - Sync the chores header to the **final** commit title
158	    if the cycle's scope shifted; update every anchor
159	    back-reference.
160	  - Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
161	    commits; optional `### Outcome` notes.
162	  - Replace `## In Progress` with
163	    `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
164	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
165	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
166	
167	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
168	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
169	see [Pushing](#pushing).
170	
171	## Numbering
172	
173	Each commit's phase is encoded in the version suffix — `-0`
174	Preparation, `-1`/`-2`/… Work, bare `X.Y.Z` Close-out,
175	recursively for sub-cycles. The full scheme — disambiguation,
176	nesting, optional Preparation, the project's version-of-record
177	format, and the per-phase bump — lives in
178	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering), which is the
179	single source of truth for this repo's versioning.
180	
181	## Per-commit flow
182	
183	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
184	through:
185	
186	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
187	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`.
188	2. **Do the work** (see [Iterative work](#iterative-work)
189	   for the loop-and-squash technique).
190	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
191	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit.
192	4. **Validate the artifact** — a medium-specific step, skip-able
193	   for notes-only commits, mandatory at close-out. For the Rust
194	   example the cargo cycle is:
195	   1. `cargo fmt`
196	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
197	   3. `cargo test`
198	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
199	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
200	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
201	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
202	   changes and we iterate until complete.
203	6. **Write the commit description** — see
204	   [Commit description](#commit-description).
205	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
206	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
207	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
208	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
209	   keeps the verb visible):
210	
211	   ```
212	   jj commit -m \
213	   "<type>: <short description>" \
214	   -m "<intro paragraph>
215	
216	   - file1: gist
217	   - file2: gist
218	
219	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
220	   -R .
221	   ```
222	
223	**Two overrides apply:**
224	
225	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
226	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
227	  surface it; don't push through.
228	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
229	  a review or question forward.
230	
231	## Commit description
232	
233	[Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/):
234	
235	```
236	<type>: <short description>
237	```
238	
239	Titles carry **no trailing `(<version>)` suffix**. The
240	version-of-record — where it lives and its bump cadence, see
241	[versioning.md](versioning.md) — is useful for confirming
242	you're running the version you're testing, not a per-commit
243	title marker. We think per-commit version-in-title is
244	unstable on large projects: many changes are in flight
245	simultaneously, and a
246	version is only stable once merged into the main repo — so
247	titles don't carry one.
248	
249	### Title
250	
251	- ≤50 chars total.
252	- Common types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `test`,
253	  `docs`, `chore`.
254	- Favor terse phrasings.
255	- **Distinct per step** — each of a cycle's commits gets its
256	  own descriptive title (no shared cycle title with a step
257	  marker). Share a greppable stem across the cycle's titles
258	  (e.g. `ring buffer`) so `git log --grep` collects them; the
259	  chores section header matches the close-out title. See
260	  AGENTS.md
261	  [Conventional-commit shape](../AGENTS.md#conventional-commit-shape-ladder--chores--commit).
262	
263	### Body
264	
265	[Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
266	wrap ≤72. Bullet content differs per repo:
267	
268	- **App-repo body**: file-by-file. One bullet per file
269	  changed (file plus a one-line gist). Sub-bullets for
270	  files with multiple distinct changes:
271	
272	  ```
273	  - path/to/file1
274	    - first distinct change
275	    - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
276	       with continuation indented 5
277	  ```
278	
279	  The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
280	  cycle's mechanical change record. Chores carries the
281	  narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
282	  "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###` subsection.
283	
284	- **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: bullets describe
285	  in-session activity rather than code changes.
286	
287	### Trailer
288	
289	`ochid:` as the last line of the body — see
290	[Cross-repo linking (ochid trailers)](../AGENTS.md#cross-repo-linking-ochid-trailers)
291	in AGENTS.md for the convention.
292	
293	For breaking changes, use the hyphenated `BREAKING-CHANGE:`
294	trailer key. `BREAKING CHANGE:` (with a space) is the only
295	space-separated key the Conventional Commits spec allows; the
296	hyphenated form is also valid and avoids the space ambiguity.
297	
298	## Reviewing changes
299	
300	Work review looks at the **uncommitted working-copy diff**,
301	on the way to commit. The user opens diffs in their
302	editor (Zed, VSCode); jj commands are for terminal:
303	
304	- `jj diff` — working-copy diff (uncommitted)
305	- `jj diff -r @-` — diff of the previous commit
306	- `jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>` — any two revisions
307	- `jj show -r <X>` — description + diff for one rev
308	
309	Don't `jj edit -r @-` to view a past commit — that marks
310	it mutable and shifts `@`; use `jj diff -r @-` or
311	`jj show -r @-`.
312	
313	See [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders) for the
314	close-out squash recipe and recovery; revset primitives
315	are in [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets).
316	
317	## Pushing
318	
319	### Policy
320	
321	Push is **discretionary** during the cycle (backup,
322	progress visibility) and **mandatory at close-out** —
323	the cycle's result must be published.
324	
325	**Approval is per-push.** Every push — any repo, any kind:
326	cycle push, interim backup, recovery/surgery force-push —
327	happens only after the user has reviewed the changes to be
328	published and explicitly approved that specific push.
329	Approval of a plan that *includes* a push does not authorize
330	the push itself; stop and ask again at the moment of pushing.
331	
332	**Default is interactive; an explicit scoped delegation waives
333	the gates.** The gates above — per-push approval, the
334	commit-description review (show title+body and stop), and the
335	hard stop after push/finalize — are the *interactive default*.
336	They yield when the user **explicitly** delegates a complete,
337	bounded task and authorizes carrying it through ("do all of X
338	and push each step, don't check in"). The bot then proceeds
339	through that task's commits and pushes without stopping, and
340	continues past each push to the next step. Conditions:
341	
342	- **Explicit grant** — never inferred from a task merely being
343	  well-scoped; the user's words must authorize unattended
344	  completion. "Commit and push" (or "then push") names the
345	  destination, not a waiver: it authorizes the push *after*
346	  the normal work review and description review, not skipping
347	  them. Only wording that explicitly waives the stops ("don't
348	  check in", "no need to review", "carry it through
349	  unattended") waives them.
350	- **Bounded goal** — covers the named task only; does not carry
351	  to the next task or a vaguer follow-on.
352	- **Destructive ops still pause** — delegation covers the task's
353	  ordinary commits and pushes; it does *not* pre-authorize a
354	  genuinely irreversible action (force-push over published
355	  history, history rewrite, deleting a remote branch). Those can
356	  permanently destroy work and aren't a normal cycle step, so the
357	  bot flags one before acting. An ordinary delegated cycle never
358	  reaches this.
359	- **Still transparent** — report each commit/push as it lands
360	  (title + outcome) so the user can catch up.
361	- **When in doubt, ask** — ambiguous authorization falls back to
362	  per-push approval.
363	
364	### Shape at close-out push
365	
366	At close-out the cycle's *work* is done; its *published
367	shape* is the remaining choice, made at push time. Surface
368	the options and get user approval before pushing — once on
369	the target, changing shape is a remote rewrite (force-push,
370	needs approval), so choose deliberately.
371	
372	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target.
373	  Right for straightforward changes where the Work-N is
374	  focused on one or two files.
375	- **Merge non-ff** *(current default)* — `main` gains a
376	  merge commit (`X.Y.Z`); cycle commits stay reachable via
377	  two parents. `jj log -r ..@ -n <N>` shows the trapezoidal
378	  shape. See [Merge non-ff recipe](#merge-non-ff-recipe)
379	  for the full setup sequence.
380	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on
381	  `main`. Use when the decomposition itself is
382	  informative. Each chores section keeps its own header /
383	  `Commits:` ref; no consolidation churn.
384	
385	Set up squash/merge before invoking `vc-x1 push`; use
386	`jj git push` directly for non-standard shapes.
387	
388	### Merge non-ff recipe
389	
390	Setting up a [Merge non-ff](#shape-at-close-out-push)
391	close-out is a fixed sequence. `<closeout>` is the cycle's
392	close-out commit, `<prev>` the previous cycle's close-out
393	(the current `main` tip), `<work-tip>` the cycle's last
394	Work commit:
395	
396	1. **Rebase the close-out into a merge** — `jj rebase -r
397	   <closeout> --onto <prev> --onto <work-tip>`.
398	   - `-r <closeout>` keeps the `<closeout>` commit in place.
399	   - `--onto <prev>` becomes its first parent (trunk).
400	   - `--onto <work-tip>` becomes the second parent.
401	
402	   Together these make `<closeout>` a merge of
403	   `<prev>` + `<work-tip>`, forming a trapezoidal commit.
404	2. **Use `jj new <merge>`** to add an empty `@` above the
405	   merge. The rebase left `@` *on* the now-content-bearing
406	   merge, which git/IDE diff views show as uncommitted;
407	   `jj new` restores the clean empty `@` on top.
408	3. **Push** — `jj git push --bookmark main -R .`.
409	
410	**Post-hoc caveat.** If the cycle was already pushed
411	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) its commits are
412	immutable: the rebase needs `--ignore-immutable` and the
413	push force-updates `main`.
414	The standard sequence assumes the merge is set up *before*
415	the close-out push.
416	
417	### vc-x1 push wrapper
418	
419	`vc-x1 push <bookmark>` wraps per-push mechanics. See
420	`vc-x1 push --help` for current flags.
421	
422	**Current limitation**: only fully supports the
423	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) shape; other
424	shapes need manual jj steps. Improvements tracked /
425	planned:
426	
427	- N:1 code↔bot for Merge non-ff (`## Todo` entry P1).
428	- Symmetric squash (planned, to be captured in `-2`).
429	- Per-repo bookmark names (planned).
430	
431	### .claude cadence
432	
433	**Cadence**: one push = one `.claude` commit, paired
434	with every code commit in that push.
435	
436	The `.claude` working copy accumulates session data
437	across the cycle; its change ID stays stable across
438	snapshots, `jj describe`, and the finalize commit, so
439	code-side `ochid:` trailers resolve.
440	
441	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives
442	on `main`, regardless of the app-side bookmark. **Do not
443	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror
444	app-side branches** — risks the bot steering session
445	pushes to the wrong remote ref.
446	
447	Ending a session: if the user runs `/exit` there will be
448	session information created, which we don't worry about.
449	The user can close the terminal instead and `@` will
450	remain empty.
451	
452	### Bot communication at the reviews
453	
454	Use plain prose — no insider jargon ("Gate N signal",
455	"Checkpoint N", etc.):
456	
457	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
458	  "Work complete. Please review."
459	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE`
460	  and `$BODY` explicitly; ask permission to commit/push.
461	  Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push ... --title ...
462	  --body ...` invocation by default.
463	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
464	  options (squash / merge / keep) and the push target;
465	  wait for the user's choice before any `jj squash` /
466	  `jj rebase` / `jj git push` invocation.
467	
468	### After push or finalize: stop and wait
469	
470	After you **push** (cross the remote boundary) or launch
471	**finalize** on the `.claude` repo (`vc-x1 finalize --repo
472	.claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach`) — by
473	hand or via the `vc-x1 push` wrapper — you **MUST NEVER**
474	proceed (next step, edit, tool call, text output) until the
475	user explicitly directs you to continue. **Even when the next
476	step seems obvious — wait.** Treat push-or-finalize as a hard
477	stop for the whole turn.
478	
479	`vc-x1 push` performs both as its tail stages — `push-app`
480	then the detached `vc-x1 finalize` on `.claude` — so there is
481	no gap left to speak in once the push is invoked. Put **all**
482	closing words *before* invoking it. The harness rejects an empty
483	turn, so it may force a visible token after the tool returns; if
484	so, emit a bare acknowledgment only (e.g. "landed") — never a
485	summary, verification, or next-step offer.
486	
487	Why (`.claude` is the live journal finalize snapshots →
488	squashes → pushes):
489	
490	- **`--detach`**: lets the tool return immediately so the
491	  harness can keep flushing the *trailing* session-data — the
492	  tool call's own transcript and any words emitted *before*
493	  the invoke — into the snapshot.
494	- **`--delay 10`**: gives the harness time to complete that
495	  flush before the snapshot is taken. It is **not** time for
496	  the bot to do more work.
497	- **silence**: the bot must not summarize or perform any work
498	  after the tool returns. The harness may force a visible token
499	  (it rejects an empty turn) — keep it to a bare acknowledgment,
500	  never a summary or a next step.
501	
502	**Known slip**: the bot has emitted a summary after launching
503	finalize. There is no "harmless" closing line — if it's worth
504	saying, say it *before* the invoke.
505	
506	### Recovery
507	
508	- **If push exits before `finalize-claude`** (e.g.
509	  failure between `push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run
510	  finalize by hand:
511	
512	  ```
513	  vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
514	  ```
515	
516	- **Run finalize again if `@` is non-empty** after the
517	  finalize's squash-and-commit (also desirable after
518	  extra activity by the bot's agents).
519	  - Why: finalize snapshots `.claude` at `--delay`, but the
520	    bot keeps writing session data while it runs — the tool's
521	    own invocation plus the bot's closing response land
522	    *after* the snapshot.
523	  - Safe to repeat: bot session data is append-only, so a
524	    re-run never conflicts or overwrites. (This could
525	    change; it is not under the user's control.)
526	  - Fix: run `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push
527	    <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach` another time to capture
528	    that tail; one extra pass is generally enough.
529	  - No guarantees: events outside the bot's control can leave
530	    `@` non-empty — e.g. the bot's back end may decide to
531	    squash/consolidate session data, which can take minutes
532	    and land after the snapshot. The remedy is the same: just
533	    run finalize again. This is why a single pass is never
534	    guaranteed to leave `@` empty.
535	- **Clear push's saved state** after any out-of-band
536	  recovery — `rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push
537	  <bookmark> --restart` — otherwise push resumes from a
538	  stale stage.
539	- **Late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded**
540	  (e.g. updating AGENTS.md or memory) requires `jj
541	  squash --ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a
542	  remote rewrite and needs explicit approval like any
543	  push.
544	
545	## Iterative work
546	
547	When work for a single commit (the **target**) benefits
548	from incremental review, loop:
549	
550	1. `jj new -R .` — fresh empty `@` on top of the target.
551	2. Make the next round of changes.
552	3. User reviews the round (see
553	   [Reviewing changes](#reviewing-changes)).
554	4. `jj squash -R .` folds into the target and creates a
555	   new empty `@`.
556	5. If not done, go to step 2.
557	
558	Same jj mechanics as a
559	[sub-cycle ladder](#sub-cycle-ladders), but at
560	single-commit scope — the version
561	doesn't change.
562	
563	## Sub-cycle ladders
564	
565	When a Work commit subdivides into a sub-cycle (see
566	[Numbering](#numbering) for suffix nesting), its Work
567	commits typically live as a local jj `@` chain and
568	**collapse into the sub-cycle's Close-out** before the
569	parent cycle continues. Ladder commits are scratch —
570	for review and bisection only.
571	
572	### Per-Work-commit contract within a ladder
573	
574	For each Work commit in the ladder:
575	
576	1. `jj new -R .` — create a fresh empty `@`.
577	2. Do the commit's work.
578	3. Run the fast validation (Rust example: `cargo test
579	   --bins`). **Non-negotiable** — for code, build and clippy
580	   alone miss regressions until a later commit runs the full
581	   suite, raising bisection cost.
582	4. `jj describe -m "..." -m "..." -R .` — working title
583	   only; the sub-cycle Close-out collects everything
584	   into one final commit.
585	
586	### Navigating the ladder
587	
588	Common moves:
589	
590	- `jj log -r '<base>::' -R .` — see the whole ladder
591	  from its base.
592	- `jj edit -r <prefix> -R .` — jump `@` to any ladder
593	  commit by chid prefix; useful for bisection.
594	- `jj edit @-- -R .` — quick-jump back two commits.
595	- `jj diff -r <chid> -R .` — review one commit in
596	  isolation.
597	
598	Modifications to any ladder commit rewrite it in place;
599	descendants auto-rebase.
600	
601	### Close-out: squash the ladder
602	
603	When all ladder Work commits are done and tests pass:
604	
605	```
606	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
607	```
608	
609	`<base>` is the parent of the first ladder commit; `-u`
610	keeps `@`'s description and discards the sources'.
611	After squash, history is linear: `<base> → @`;
612	intermediate commits are auto-abandoned.
613	
614	Then `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` as for any other commit.
615	
616	For N = 1 the squash is a no-op (`<base>..@-` is empty
617	when `@-` is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
618	
619	### Recovery
620	
621	If a ladder commit goes wrong, back out without losing
622	prior commits:
623	
624	- **Discard the current commit.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
625	  drops it; you get a fresh empty `@` on the same
626	  parent.
627	- **Edit an earlier commit.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
628	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-ladder-chid>`
629	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
630	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows
631	  the op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts
632	  to that point. Full undo — removes *all* ladder work
633	  after the chosen op. Use only to start over.
634	
635	# References
636	
637	- [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets) — revset primitives
638	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
639	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
640	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
641	  for squash-recipe experiments.
642	- The per-commit `cargo test --bins` gate exists because a
643	  regression introduced in an early ladder commit can go
644	  uncaught until a later commit runs the full suite, raising
645	  bisection cost.
646	
```

1	# Versioning
2	
3	How this project versions its commits and the running
4	artifact. The scheme — the cycle step-numbering and the
5	unique-per-commit aim — is generic and shared across projects:
6	this file is copied **verbatim**, with
7	[Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
8	covering each medium by conditional rather than per-project
9	edits.
10	
11	## Terms
12	
13	Three names, used as defined here across
14	[AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md),
15	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md), and the notes files:
16	
17	- **version** — the per-commit version (e.g. `0.3.0-5.3.0`),
18	  written with `-` in prose, ladders, chores, todo, and commit
19	  talk; its suffix encodes the cycle phase (see
20	  [Step numbering](#step-numbering)).
21	- **version-of-record** — the authoritative stored copy of the
22	  version, in the project's manifest (see
23	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record));
24	  a built or running artifact derives from it.
25	- **versioning** — the topic: this scheme as a whole.
26	
27	## Recording the version-of-record
28	
29	Where the version-of-record lives, how it's stored and
30	surfaced, and how often it changes — pick the case that fits
31	your medium:
32	
33	- **Manifest** — where the version-of-record is stored:
34	  - if Rust, `Cargo.toml` `[package].version`
35	  - if Python, `pyproject.toml` `[project].version` (or the
36	    committed config it's sourced from)
37	  - otherwise wherever the medium records it (a generic
38	    `version.toml`, a book's frontmatter, …) — add the case as
39	    needed
40	- **Notation** — how the `-` form is stored:
41	  - if the format allows `-` (TOML `version.toml`, `Cargo.toml`),
42	    store it verbatim
43	  - if it bars `-` (PEP 440's local segment, e.g. a Python
44	    project), remap to `+` — `0.3.0-5.3.0` → `0.3.0+5.3.0`: same
45	    version, just the stored spelling
46	- **Reporter** — how a built artifact surfaces the
47	  version-of-record:
48	  - if a CLI app, `<cli-app> -V`
49	  - if a TUI/GUI, add to Help/About or display on the title
50	- **Cadence** — how often to bump: see
51	  [Unique per commit](#unique-per-commit-preference-not-requirement);
52	  this project follows the per-commit preference.
53	
54	## Unique per commit (preference, not requirement)
55	
56	Our general notion is that the version-of-record should change
57	on **every commit**, so a built or running artifact identifies
58	the exact commit it came from.
59	
60	- This is a preference, **not** a hard requirement. A project
61	  following the cycle protocol may bump less often — once per
62	  cycle, only at release, and so on. Record the choice in
63	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
64	  if it differs.
65	- It is achievable because the cycle's versions (below) are
66	  **pre-assignable**, unlike the git SHA, which a commit cannot
67	  contain (see the cycle protocol's
68	  [Commits backfill](cycle-protocol.md#commits-backfill)).
69	
70	## Step numbering
71	
72	The cycle (Preparation -> Work -> Close-out; see
73	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md)) encodes each commit's
74	phase in the version suffix — the **final identifier `0` marks
75	a Preparation**:
76	
77	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
78	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
79	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
80	
81	**Preparation is optional.** A lightweight cycle — no ladder,
82	no setup commit — skips `-0` and starts at `-1` (its first
83	Work commit). The same holds at every level: a sub-cycle
84	needing no Preparation omits its `.0` (see Nesting). One that
85	grows a Preparation later adds the `0` step without
86	renumbering siblings.
87	
88	Disambiguation:
89	
90	- `-10` — Work commit #10 (final identifier `10`), not a
91	  Preparation.
92	- `-1.0` — Preparation of the `-1` sub-cycle (final
93	  identifier `0`).
94	
95	**Nesting.** Sub-cycles append another level, recursively:
96	
97	- `X.Y.Z-3.0` — Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle
98	- `X.Y.Z-3.1`, `X.Y.Z-3.2` — its Work
99	- `X.Y.Z-3` — its Close-out
100	- `X.Y.Z-3.1.0` — Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle
101	
102	Bump the version-of-record at the start of each phase, so the
103	active phase is recorded — and, per the preference above,
104	every commit carries a distinct version.
105	
```

## feat: ring + pool demo binary

Commits: [[21]]

A runnable, visible proof for library visitors: both
primitives working across threads with throughput printed.

- `src/bin/zc-ring-x1-demo.rs`: a binary (not an example)
  so `cargo install --path . --locked` works and `-V`
  prints the version-of-record — you always know which
  build you are testing. Ring part (SPSC in-place messages,
  producer → consumer threads) and pool part (allocator
  thread fills BufSlots, freer thread frees; guards cross a
  std channel per the usage model's "send = move the
  guard").
- Prints msgs/sec per part — an eyeball smoke-baseline, not
  a benchmark (iiac-perf owns real measurement). The pool
  part's rate is channel-dominated, which is itself the
  argument for the descriptor-queue cycle; the demo says so.

- Pool ≈ malloc single-threaded (~10 ns) is the expected
  result, not a defeat: malloc's hot path is the same
  thread-local LIFO free-list trick. The pool's case is

## feat: demo alloc/free perf loops

Commits:

Two single-thread loops join the demo scoreboard: the
pool's raw alloc→write→free cost, and the same loop through
the global allocator (Box::new/drop), black_box-guarded.

- Pool ≈ malloc single-threaded (~10 ns) is the expected
  result, not a defeat: malloc's hot path is the same
  thread-local LIFO free-list trick. The pool's case is
  shared-memory zero-copy, no_std, no syscalls ever, and
  determinism for the embedded floor — capabilities, not a
  tight-loop race.
- The scoreboard now decomposes the story: ring transfer
  tens of ns, pool ops ~10 ns, std channel hundreds — the
  descriptor-queue cycle's projected win is readable off
  the four lines.

  "new_string": "- The scoreboard now decomposes the story: ring transfer\n  tens of ns, pool ops ~10 ns, std channel hundreds — the\n  descriptor-queue cycle's projected win is readable off\n  the four lines.\n\n## feat: demo cpu-pinned placement variants\n\nCommits:\n\nCross-thread numbers swung ~4× run to run because the\nscheduler decides where the two threads land. Every demo\nline now states its thread placement, and the 2t parts run\na placement ladder: unpinned, same physical core (SMT\nsiblings, shared L1/L2), different physical cores\n(preferring one outside cpu0's L3 group — the far end).\n\n- Pinning is `sched_setaffinity` via a Linux-only `libc`\n  dependency; cpu pairs are discovered at runtime from\n  `/sys/devices/system/cpu` topology (SMT sibling list, L3\n  `shared_cpu_list`), so the demo adapts to any Linux box\n  and prints a \"skipped\" line when the machine lacks the\n  shape. Non-Linux builds compile with pinning stubbed out.\n- Pinned runs execute in scoped threads that pin\n  themselves — pinning the main thread would leak its\n  affinity mask into every later part's spawned threads.\n- Functions renamed to say what each line measures:\n  `spsc_ring_one_msg_1t/_2t`, `std_mpsc_one_pool_msg_2t`\n  (std's mpsc channel carrying pool-buffer guards, used\n  SPSC), `pool_alloc_free_1t`, `global_alloc_free_1t`; the\n  1t runs pin to core 0 so every line's placement is\n  deterministic.\n- The ladder decomposes the transfer cost: same-core ~8-12\n  ns vs diff-cores ~100-300 ns for the identical ring code.\n  We think the gap is cache-line handoff distance (shared\n  L1/L2 vs cross-L3/CCD traffic), and the unpinned number\n  wandering between the two ends is scheduler placement.\n  Single runs, no mean/stdev — still an eyeball baseline,\n  not a benchmark.\n- `Msg` shrank to one word (`seq`); the torn-write canary\n  (`val`) went with it.\n\n# References"
}

```
vc-x1 0.67.0

Dual-repo commit+push+finalize in one resumable command.

Collapses today's manual Commit-Push-Finalize Flow into a
single subcommand with two interactive approval gates and a
state machine with persistent progress so interruptions can
resume without re-doing completed stages.

Stages: preflight (fmt/clippy/test) → review (approve diff)
→ message ($EDITOR / --title+--body, approve text) →
commit-app → commit-claude (skipped if clean) → bookmark-both
→ push-app → finalize-claude. Failures in commit-app /
commit-claude / bookmark-both roll both repos back via
`jj op restore` to the snapshot recorded before commit-app.
After push-app succeeds the remote boundary is crossed and
recovery is forward-only.

Non-interactive use: pass both --title and --body plus --yes
to skip the review gate. Saved state carries title/body
across resumes so only the first invocation needs them.

Usage: vc-x1 push [OPTIONS] [BOOKMARK]

Arguments:
  [BOOKMARK]
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (positional form of `--bookmark`).
          
          Accepting a positional lets the common case read as `vc-x1 push main`
          without the `--bookmark` ceremony; `--bookmark` is kept as an alias
          for scripts and for composition with other args. The two forms
          conflict if both supplied.

Options:
      --bookmark <BOOKMARK>
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (flag form; see positional)

  -V, --version
          Print the `vc-x1 X.Y.Z` banner as the first line, then continue. With
          no subcommand, prints the banner and exits.
          
          Replaces clap's auto-version (which would exit after printing): the
          banner now rides along with normal subcommand execution rather than
          gating it, so scripts can capture the version *and* the command's
          output in one invocation.

      --restart
          Clear any saved state file and start from stage 1

  -v, --verbose...
          Verbose output: -v debug, -vv trace

      --from <STAGE>
          Explicit stage to jump to (advanced / debug use)

          Possible values:
          - preflight:       Run fmt / clippy / test / install / retest
          - review:          Present diff for the first approval gate
          - message:         Compose / edit the commit message; present for
            second gate
          - commit-app:      Commit the app repo
          - commit-claude:   Commit the `.claude` session repo (skipped if
            empty)
          - bookmark-both:   Advance both bookmarks to `@-`
          - push-app:        `jj git push --bookmark <b> -R .`
          - finalize-claude: `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <b>
            ...`

      --log <LOG>
          Log file path (captures all levels)

      --step
          Pause between every stage for an interactive approval gate

      --status
          Print where the saved state thinks we are and exit

      --recheck
          Re-run `preflight` even on resume (default: skip if last run
          succeeded)

      --no-finalize
          Stop before `finalize-claude` so it can be run manually

      --dry-run
          Print the exact commands for every stage without side effects

      --title <STR>
          Commit title (skip `$EDITOR` for the message stage)

      --body <STR>
          Commit body (skip `$EDITOR` for the message stage)

  -y, --yes
          Auto-approve interactive prompts (review gate, message-edit
          confirmation). Required when `--title` / `--body` aren't both supplied
          in non-interactive contexts

  -h, --help
          Print help (see a summary with '-h')
```

```
387	### Free path: intrusive LIFO free-stack
388	
389	Freeing must be cheap for many freers and allocation must be
390	O(1) with no searching (a per-buffer available-flag makes free
391	trivial but alloc a scan). The free operation is itself the
392	marking: push the buffer onto the pool's free-stack.
393	
394	- **Intrusive** — a freed buffer's header holds the next-link
395	  as an offset; the free-list needs no extra storage.
396	- **LIFO on purpose** — we think a stack beats a free-ring on
397	  cache behavior: FIFO cycles through every buffer (maximum
398	  working set), LIFO reuses the most-recently-freed few, and
399	  the small working set also helps the TLB. The full
400	  same-thread malloc-style hot-reuse win is diluted because
401	  the freer and allocator are different cores; the
402	  working-set effect stands regardless.
403	- **Single-popper Treiber stack** — free = CAS the head to
404	  your buffer's offset (MPSC push side: any thread or process
405	  frees into any pool); alloc = the one owning allocator pops
406	  the head. A single popper eliminates classic Treiber ABA:
407	  no node leaves the list under a competing pop, pushes just
408	  retry the CAS.
409	- **Validated pops** — head and next-links are untrusted;
410	  the popper bounds/alignment-checks each offset and caps
411	  pops per attempt, so a corrupt peer can lose buffers or
412	  cycle the list but never make the allocator read outside
413	  the pool.
414	- **CAS note** — the free-stack is the first structure to
415	  need CAS; the ring protocol itself stays load/store-only,
416	  so the atomic floor rises only for pool users.
417	
418	### Pool topology and phasing
419	
420	- **One owning allocator per pool** (invariant, phase 1) — a
421	  single thread allocates; any party frees. Per-thread pools
422	  are the performance default and satisfy this trivially.
423	- **Shared pools already exist in phase 1** — shared for
424	  reading, forwarding, and freeing; only allocation is
425	  single-owner.
426	- **Shared allocation (phase 2)** — per-thread pools cost
427	  memory; N threads sharing one pool's capacity needs
428	  multi-popper alloc, which needs an ABA-proof head:
429	  pack `(offset, generation)` in one `AtomicU64` and CAS
430	  both together. A contained per-pool variant (opt-in flag),
431	  not a redesign; hot paths keep the cheap single-popper
432	  version. Heterogeneity mitigates meanwhile: a small private
433	  hot-path pool plus a big shared fallback pool.
434	- **MPSC ring (future sibling)** — multi-producer queues need
435	  a different ring protocol (CAS-claimed producer index,
436	  per-slot sequence state); it slots in as a sibling
437	  primitive under the same descriptor layer. The endpoint
438	  claims word should model role slots, not a single producer
439	  bit, so N producer claims fit later without a layout
440	  rethink.
441	
442	### Usage model: roles and buffer lifecycle
443	
444	The user-facing contract — who may do what, per object and
445	per buffer state — lives in the README:
446	[Usage model](../README.md#usage-model-roles-and-buffer-lifecycle).
447	The tests exercise exactly what it permits. Summary: ring =
448	one producer + one consumer; pool = one allocator + any
449	freers; a buffer is always in exactly one state
450	(free / allocated / in-flight [vacant until descriptor
451	queues] / freed) with one permitted toucher; "send" this
452	cycle means moving the `BufSlot`.
453	
454	### Overflow FIFO (future)
455	
456	When a queue's ring is Full, the sender appends the message to
457	a pending FIFO instead of failing — "send" then always
458	succeeds while pool memory lasts.
459	
460	- **Intrusive, zero-allocation** — the FIFO links through the
461	  same embedded next-link offset the free-stack uses. A
462	  buffer is on at most one list at a time (free stack,
463	  pending FIFO, in flight in a ring, held by an owner), so
464	  one link field serves every state.
465	- **Sender-private** — the pending list belongs to the
466	  producer endpoint: head + tail offsets for O(1) append, no
467	  shared mutation, no CAS. Order discipline: the producer
468	  drains the FIFO oldest-first before ring-sending anything
469	  new, or FIFO order breaks. Draining happens on subsequent
470	  send attempts and/or an explicit flush.
471	- **Validated traversal** — the links live in shared pool
472	  memory a peer can scribble; the drainer bounds- and
473	  alignment-checks each offset as the free-stack popper does.
474	- **Naturally bounded** — messages come from pools, so the
475	  FIFO cannot outgrow total pool capacity; backpressure
476	  reappears as allocation failure rather than queue Full.
477	
478	### Prior art: iceoryx2
479	
480	[iceoryx2](https://github.com/eclipse-iceoryx/iceoryx2) is a
481	Rust-native zero-copy IPC library implementing most of this
482	layer: publishers loan uninitialized samples from a pool
483	allocator, write in place, and send later or never (get/send
484	decoupled); receivers get offsets they resolve against their
485	own mappings; events, request-response, and multi-producer
486	patterns exist. Study its loan/send API and pool-offset
487	machinery before implementing the pools — its lessons are
488	cheaper stolen than rediscovered.
489	
490	What keeps this project distinct from it:
491	
492	- iceoryx2 is service-oriented middleware (discovery, naming,
493	  configuration) for Linux/macOS/Windows/QNX-class platforms;
494	  this crate is a small set of composable primitives.
495	- No `no_std` / load-store-only floor — our ring reaches
496	  `thumbv6m`, with CAS confined to the pool layer.
497	- Trust posture: our attach-validated
498	  hostile-peer-cannot-cause-UB discipline versus cooperating
499	  participants within a framework.
500	
501	### Open questions
502	
503	- **Pool-id allocation** — coordinator-assigned versus derived
504	  from the shm object's identity.
505	- **Setup plane** — how a process discovers and maps a pool it
506	  hasn't seen: pre-arranged at init (embedded-friendly,
507	  allocation-free after startup) versus fd-passing over a
508	  Unix socket (Linux-friendly, dynamic); likely both, as
509	  profiles.
510	- **Message header shape** — minimum provenance is
511	  `(pool id, offset)`; whether length or a type tag joins it
512	  is undecided. Settle this early — the embedded next-link
513	  is load-bearing for three states (free-stack, pending
514	  FIFO, future lists), so the link, provenance, and any
515	  length/type tag should be laid out together before the
516	  first pool implementation lands.
517	
518	## Resolved questions
519	
520	- **Cross-process trust** — resolved in 0.3.0-4: the
521	  all-atomic header plus the geometry snapshot mean a hostile
522	  peer cannot cause UB, only garbage messages (still valid
523	  `T`s — zerocopy guarantees representation, not sense) or
524	  spurious Full/Empty. `attach` remains `unsafe` for the
525	  mapping-liveness contract, not for peer trust.
526	- **`split()` once-guard** — direction chosen: an
527	  endpoint-claims word in the header (CAS to claim the
528	  producer / consumer role, second claimant gets an error),
529	  which also catches in-process double-attach; costs a
530	  layout_version bump (or spends `_pad0`). Promoted to a
531	  `## Todo` entry in [todo.md](todo.md); the documented
532	  contract stands until then.
533	
```

> AGENT

`-1` is marked current. This commit's work is settling the design, so before I write anything into `ring-buffer-design.md`, here's the proposal — four questions, each with my recommendation. This doubles as the walkthrough you wanted.

**1. Descriptor shape: index, not byte offset — `Desc { pool_id: u32, buf_idx: u32 }` (8 bytes, POD).**
The design doc says "(pool id, buffer offset)", but the doc's real requirement is "offsets only, everywhere" — meaning *no pointers* in shared structures, because each process maps the pool at a different address. A buffer **index** satisfies that just as well as a byte offset, and it's strictly simpler: it's what the free-stack and `BufSlot.idx` already use, validation is one comparison (`idx < buf_count`), and the receiver computes the address as `bufs + idx * buf_size` from its own attached geometry. A byte offset would need bounds *and* divisibility checks to prove it lands exactly on a buffer boundary. Both u32s match the pool's existing u32 index space; 8 bytes rides easily in a 64-byte ring slot.

**2. Pool-id source: assigned by a per-process registry at register time.**
The open question was coordinator-assigned vs derived-from-shm-identity — that's the *cross-process* question, and it stays open. In-process, the registry just hands out the next table slot: `registry.register(pool) -> PoolId`. The registry is fixed-capacity (const-generic array, no_std-friendly, no allocation), holding one `Pool` handle per id. That handle is a resolver/freer handle, which the pool contract already permits — "at most one attached handle allocates, any handle may free" — so the allocating thread keeps its own `Pool` while the registry holds another over the same region.

**3. Resolve-side validation: full, always.**
A descriptor arriving through shared memory is untrusted (same posture as ring geometry): unknown `pool_id` → error; `buf_idx >= buf_count` → error; `T` geometry mismatch → same `check_type` panic as `alloc` (programming error, not hostile input). A hostile descriptor can produce a garbage-but-valid `T` or a double-free-shaped protocol violation — never UB from the resolve itself.

**4. The ownership contract — this is the one real `unsafe`:**
- `registry.into_desc(slot: BufSlot<T>) -> Desc` — **safe**. Consumes the guard (identifying the pool by the guard's header address, O(N) over a tiny table), returns the descriptor. Ownership doesn't vanish — it's now *carried by the descriptor*, exactly the "in-flight" buffer state the usage model reserved.
- `unsafe registry.resolve::<T>(desc: Desc) -> Result<BufSlot<T>, Error>` — **unsafe**, and this is the honest core: resolving mints a guard that acts as `&mut T`. The types can't prevent you from resolving the same descriptor twice (two owners, aliased `&mut`) or resolving a descriptor you invented. The caller's contract: *this descriptor came from `into_desc` and is being resolved exactly once, ownership transferred through a channel with proper ordering* (the ring's commit→reserve Release/Acquire provides that). All the bounds validation in #3 still runs — `unsafe` covers only what validation can't check: uniqueness of ownership.

The flow then reads: `pool.alloc` → write → `registry.into_desc` → ring `reserve_slot::<Desc>`/commit → … → ring reserve/release → `registry.resolve` → read → `free()` (or forward it over another queue — that's the "any message over any queue" payoff).

One deliberate deferral: the design doc's *in-buffer* provenance header ("every buffer begins with `(pool id, offset)`") is not needed while provenance travels in the descriptor itself, and the pool already landed without it. I'd record that as explicitly deferred rather than silently dropped.

If this matches your understanding — or after we've hashed out whatever doesn't — I'll write it into `ring-buffer-design.md` as the `-1` docs commit. What would you like to dig into?

> DEVELOPER

I've switched you to xhigh to put more aritifical-neurons on the task :)
So'd like you to re-evalute.

> AGENT

Re-evaluated from scratch. The core (index-based 8-byte `Desc`, per-process registry, safe `into_desc` / unsafe `resolve`) held up, but the deeper pass found one real design hole and two refinements worth making. The delta:

**1. The registry can't soundly hold a second `Pool` handle — hole fixed with a derived view.**
My sketch had the registry holding a `Pool` obtained via `Pool::attach` on the same region the allocator's `Pool::init` already exclusively borrows. In-process that's either a borrow-checker error or (via a saved raw pointer) exactly the Stacked-Borrows retag hazard `pool.rs` already warns about — `init` takes the region pointer *exactly once* by design. The fix is to derive the second handle from the first instead of from the region: `pool.resolver() -> PoolResolver` (a `&self` method copying the header ref, buffer base, and geometry — same provenance as the pool's own pointers, so no new aliasing). `PoolResolver` is the non-allocating view: it can mint guards from validated indices and identify its pool by header address; it's `Send + Sync` (its only shared-memory touch is the free CAS, already MPSC-safe), so one registry of resolvers is shared by plain `&` across both demo threads while the allocator keeps its unique `Pool`. This also composes unchanged with cross-process later — a resolver derived from an `attach`ed handle works identically.

**2. Geometry mismatch on resolve is `Err`, not the `alloc`-style panic.**
`alloc::<T>` panics on `T`-vs-pool geometry mismatch because you chose `T` against your own pool — a programming error. But `resolve::<T>(desc)` checks `T` against *whichever pool the descriptor names*, and the descriptor is untrusted input. A hostile peer choosing which pool gets compared must not be able to choose a panic (that's a DoS lever, against the hostile-peer posture). So resolve's failures are all `Err`: unknown pool id, index out of range, geometry mismatch. `unsafe` on resolve then covers only the one thing validation can't check — ownership uniqueness: *this desc came from `into_desc`, arrived over a happens-before-establishing channel (the ring's commit→reserve gives this), and is resolved exactly once.*

**3. `into_desc` returns the guard back on failure.**
`registry.into_desc(slot)` consumes the guard; if the slot's pool isn't registered, returning a bare error would strand the buffer (guards leak on drop, by design). So the error variant carries the `BufSlot` back: `Result<Desc, (BufSlot<T>, Error)>`-shaped. No leak paths introduced by the new API.

Two confirmations from the re-check worth stating: `Desc` **must** be plain copyable data — `FromBytes` means the ring consumer literally mints one from shared bytes, so there's no point making it a move-only token; ownership discipline lives entirely in resolve's contract. And the in-buffer provenance header stays deferred cleanly: in this design the descriptor *is* the message's travel form (forwarding re-sends the desc), so no flow exists where a buffer travels without its provenance — the deferral costs nothing until someone invents one, and adding it later is a pool `layout_version` bump.

The ladder holds as written (`-2` grows `Pool::resolver()` but the title still fits). If this version matches your understanding, I'll write it into `ring-buffer-design.md` as the `-1` docs commit.

> DEVELOPER

Makes some sense, but some observations/constraints:
- In the simplest case an embedded system with no virtual or shared memory,
  I think the descriptor can boil down to a pool_id maybe the buf_idx could both be pointers.
- We haven't touched on message formats other than using Google Zerocopy transmut
  capabilities, but in the real world with multiple messages each message is
  going to have an id which will drive a match statement or .. to know which
  message we have. Shouldn't matter at this level but a demo use of this will
  need something like that.
- Not now but I'm wondering how trait's might be use to allow different usage styles,
  maybe a trait could handle the boiler plate in a "casting" a buffer to an Instance
  of a message?

> AGENT

All three observations are sound — here's how each lands in the design, and then I've written the `-1` doc. 

**Embedded pointer descriptors**: agreed that's the degenerate form — no MMU, one trust domain, the descriptor could just be a flattened guard (pool ptr + buf ptr) and resolve becomes a cast. I'm keeping `(pool_id, buf_idx)` as the *canonical* form though, for two reasons: a pointer can't be validated against anything (a scribbled pointer descriptor is UB on deref, where id+index degrades to `Err` — the crate's corruption posture), and the id form's resolve cost is already near-pointer-cheap (array index + two bounds checks, no search). The pointer profile gets recorded as a future opt-in, which connects to your traits point. Your observation also improved the design: `into_desc` now takes the `PoolId` explicitly (the sender got it from `register`), making it O(1) with a header-identity check catching mispairing, instead of my O(N) scan.

**Message ids / dispatch**: agreed, and it's cleanly out of this layer — the descriptor is deliberately type-agnostic; the tag that drives the receiver's `match` is *payload*-level (first word of the message, or `TryFromBytes` tagged enums). The design doc's open "message header shape" question gets half-settled: provenance travels in the descriptor, type tag is payload-level, in-buffer header deferred. The demo will carry a minimal first-word tag so the flow looks real.

**Traits for usage styles**: parked in `## Ideas` — a `Message` trait (const `MSG_ID` + the zerocopy bounds) hiding the cast boilerplate, with a possible transport seam the pointer-descriptor profile could slot in behind.

Now writing the design into the notes:

The error side hands the\n  guard back, so a miss cannot leak the buffer.\n- **`unsafe resolve::<T>(desc) -> Result<BufSlot<T>, _>`**\n  — every failure is an `Err`, never a panic: unknown pool\n  id, index out of range, `T` geometry mismatch. > AGENT

Added as `### System model (overview)` at the top of the messaging layer, before Layer requirements. The outline:

- A process may have one or more pools; each pool has one owning allocator (a single thread allocates; any holder frees).
- Not all pools in a process need to be in shared memory — a private pool serves intra-process messaging.
- A message to be sent to another process must come from a pool in shared memory, and the receiver must have that pool mapped.
- Queues carry descriptors, not payloads: a uniform small `(pool id, buffer index)` regardless of message size; the payload stays at rest in its pool buffer.
- Any message travels over any queue, provided both endpoints have the message's pool registered.
- Each process keeps its own registry mapping pool ids to that process's view (mapping) of each pool.
- Ownership follows the descriptor: alloc → write → send the descriptor over a queue → the receiver owns it → read, forward over another queue, or free. A buffer is always in exactly one state with one permitted toucher.
- Any holder — any thread or process — may free; the free pushes the buffer directly onto its pool's free-stack in shared memory (no round-trip through the owning process), where the owning allocator finds it on a later alloc.
- Descriptors and free-stack links arriving through shared memory are untrusted: validated before use; corruption degrades to errors or lost buffers, never UB.
- 0.7.0 builds the in-process slice of this model; cross-process setup (mapping exchange, pool-id coordination) stays design.

> AGENT

Worth doing, but selectively — here's my thinking.

You've spotted the real limitation: in GitHub markdown, headings are the *only* robustly linkable unit. A bullet has no anchor, so when a section covers five distinct topics as bullets, every inbound link lands at the section top and the reader scans. Promoting sub-topics to `####` headings fixes that — GitHub auto-generates anchors at every heading level, `#####` included.

The counterweight is fragmentation and anchor churn. The design doc's sections are mostly a screenful; landing at the top loses little there. And every heading you mint is an anchor contract — rename it and inbound links dangle (the checklist catches them, but it's churn). Turning every bullet into a `####` would make the doc read like an index rather than a narrative.

So the rule I'd adopt: **promote to a heading when two or more inbound links want different spots inside one section** — multiplicity of targets, not linkability in principle, is the signal. Applying that to the overview's current links:

- `#provenance-and-descriptors` — 3 links wanting different bullets (shared-memory precondition, descriptor shape, trust). This is the one genuine case: split its bullets into `####` subsections (Offsets only / Message provenance / Descriptors / Pool self-description / Pool-id resolution / Trust — or a slightly coarser grouping).
- `#descriptor-and-registry-design-070` — 3 links, but two of them ("registry", "0.7.0 slice") genuinely mean the whole section; I'd leave it intact.
- `#layer-requirements` — 2 links, section is ~15 lines; leave it.

`#####` I'd hold in reserve — nothing currently has two levels of finer detail, and deep nesting past `####` usually means the section wants to be its own `###` instead.

Want me to do the Provenance split now as a small docs commit before `-2`, fold it into `-2`'s commit when that lands, or just record the rule and apply it when a link actually annoys?

417	### Provenance and descriptors
418	
419	- **Offsets only, everywhere** — different per-process
420	  mappings mean no pointers in any shared structure; this
421	  also serves the single-address-space case unchanged.
422	- **Message provenance** — every buffer begins with a small
423	  header naming its pool `(pool id, offset)`; whoever holds a
424	  message last must free it to the right pool, so provenance
425	  travels with the message.
426	- **Descriptors** — a queue entry is a uniform small
427	  `(pool id, buffer index)` regardless of message size; the
428	  receiver resolves the pool id and learns geometry from the
429	  pool's own header (index over byte offset settled in
430	  [Descriptor and registry design](#descriptor-and-registry-design-070)).
431	- **Pool self-description** — each pool region starts with a
432	  header (magic, layout version, buffer size, count,
433	  alignment), attach-validated exactly like the ring header.
434	- **Pool-id resolution** — each process keeps a local registry
435	  mapping pool id to its own mapping of that pool's region.
436	  "Any message over any queue" carries the precondition that
437	  both endpoints have the message's pool mapped.
438	- **Trust** — offsets and indices arriving through shared
439	  memory are untrusted: bounds- and alignment-checked against
440	  the receiver's own view before use, same discipline as the
441	  ring's geometry snapshot.
442

  "old_string": "### Provenance and descriptors\n\n- **Offsets only, everywhere** — different per-process\n  mappings mean no pointers in any shared structure; this\n  also serves the single-address-space case unchanged.\n- **Message provenance** — every buffer begins with a small\n  header naming its pool `(pool id, offset)`; whoever holds a\n  message last must free it to the right pool, so provenance\n  travels with the message.\n- **Descriptors** — a queue entry is a uniform small\n  `(pool id, buffer index)` regardless of message size; the\n  receiver resolves the pool id and learns geometry from the\n  pool's own header (index over byte offset settled in\n  [Descriptor and registry design](#descriptor-and-registry-design-070)).\n- **Pool self-description** — each pool region starts with a\n  header (magic, layout version, buffer size, count,\n  alignment), attach-validated exactly like the ring header.\n- **Pool-id resolution** — each process keeps a local registry\n  mapping pool id to its own mapping of that pool's region.\n  \"Any message over any queue\" carries the precondition that\n  both endpoints have the message's pool mapped.\n- **Trust** — offsets and indices arriving through shared\n  memory are untrusted: bounds- and alignment-checked against\n  the receiver's own view before use, same discipline as the\n  ring's geometry snapshot.",
  "new_string": "### Provenance and descriptors\n\nSub-topics promoted to `####` headings so each is directly\nlinkable (two or more inbound links wanted different spots\ninside this section).\n\n#### Offsets only, everywhere\n\nDifferent per-process mappings mean no pointers in any\nshared structure; this also serves the single-address-space\ncase unchanged.\n\n#### Message provenance\n\nEvery buffer begins with a small header naming its pool\n`(pool id, offset)`; whoever holds a message last must free\nit to the right pool, so provenance travels with the\nmessage. (Deferred as of 0.7.0 — provenance travels in the\ndescriptor instead; see\n[Descriptor and registry design](#descriptor-and-registry-design-070).)\n\n#### Descriptors\n\nA queue entry is a uniform small `(pool id, buffer index)`\nregardless of message size; the receiver resolves the pool\nid and learns geometry from the pool's own header (index\nover byte offset settled in\n[Descriptor and registry design](#descriptor-and-registry-design-070)).\n\n#### Pool self-description\n\nEach pool region starts with a header (magic, layout\nversion, buffer size, count, alignment), attach-validated\nexactly like the ring header.\n\n#### Pool-id resolution\n\nEach process keeps a local registry mapping pool id to its\nown mapping of that pool's region. \"Any message over any\nqueue\" carries the precondition that both endpoints have\nthe message's pool mapped.\n\n#### Trust\n\nOffsets and indices arriving through shared memory are\nuntrusted: bounds- and alignment-checked against the\nreceiver's own view before use, same discipline as the\nring's geometry snapshot."
}

1	//! Message pool: fixed-size buffers over a caller-provided
2	//! region, handed out through an intrusive LIFO free-stack —
3	//! the allocation half of the messaging layer, decoupling
4	//! "get a message" from "send it" (see the design doc's
5	//! "Messaging layer: pools and descriptor queues").
6	//!
7	//! - One `#[repr(C)]` [`PoolHeader`] spanning two cache lines
8	//!   (immutable geometry, free-stack head), followed by
9	//!   `buf_count` buffers of `buf_size` bytes each.
10	//! - The free-stack is intrusive with zero per-buffer
11	//!   overhead: a *free* buffer's first word holds the index
12	//!   of the next free buffer; an allocated buffer carries no
13	//!   header at all (pool-id provenance is deferred — see
14	//!   todo.md's In Progress block).
15	//! - One owning allocator pops ([`Pool::alloc`], `&mut self`
16	//!   as the in-process single-popper token); any holder
17	//!   frees ([`BufSlot::free`], the MPSC push side). The
18	//!   [`BufSlot`] guard does not borrow the pool, so any
19	//!   number of allocated buffers may be live at once —
20	//!   the decoupling the messaging layer exists for.
21	
22	use core::marker::PhantomData;
23	use core::mem::size_of;
24	use core::ops::{Deref, DerefMut};
25	use core::sync::atomic::{AtomicU32, Ordering};
26	use zerocopy::{FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout};
27	
28	use crate::{CACHE_LINE_SIZE, CacheAligned, Error, check_type};
29	
30	/// `alloc` failed: no free buffer (or a peer-corrupted
31	/// free-stack index — validation fails toward exhaustion,
32	/// never toward handing out memory the pool doesn't own).
33	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
34	pub struct Exhausted;
35	
36	/// Layout marker written by [`Pool::init`]; rejects foreign
37	/// (including ring) regions on attach.
38	const POOL_MAGIC: u32 = 0x5A43_5031; // "ZCP1"
39	
40	/// Bumped on any change to the pool region layout.
41	const POOL_LAYOUT_VERSION: u32 = 1;
42	
43	/// Free-stack terminator: no next free buffer.
44	///
45	/// - Also why `buf_count < u32::MAX`: every valid index must
46	///   be distinguishable from the sentinel.
47	const NIL: u32 = u32::MAX;
48	
49	/// Control block at offset 0 of a pool region — two cache
50	/// lines: cold geometry, then the contended free-stack head.
51	///
52	/// - line 0: geometry, written by [`Pool::init`] with `magic`
53	///   last (`Release`), read-only thereafter. Cold: per-op
54	///   paths use the handle's snapshot.
55	/// - line 1: `first_free_idx`, CAS-contended by every freer and
56	///   the allocator — sole owner of its line.
57	/// - Every field is atomic for the same reason as the ring
58	///   [`Header`](crate::Header): a peer may be mapped at any
59	///   time, and scribbles must be garbage values, never UB.
60	#[repr(C)]
61	pub struct PoolHeader {
62	    /// Layout marker ([`POOL_MAGIC`]); stored last by init
63	    /// (`Release`), loaded first by attach (`Acquire`).
64	    magic: AtomicU32,
65	    /// Pool layout version ([`POOL_LAYOUT_VERSION`]).
66	    layout_version: AtomicU32,
67	    /// Buffer size in bytes — a [`CACHE_LINE_SIZE`] multiple.
68	    buf_size: AtomicU32,
69	    /// Buffer count — nonzero, `< u32::MAX` ([`NIL`]).
70	    buf_count: AtomicU32,
71	    /// [`CACHE_LINE_SIZE`] this region was built with; attach
72	    /// validates it like the rest of the geometry.
73	    cache_line_size: AtomicU32,
74	    /// Free-stack head: index of the first free buffer, or
75	    /// [`NIL`] when the pool is exhausted.
76	    first_free_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,
77	}
78	
79	const _: () = assert!(size_of::<PoolHeader>() == 2 * CACHE_LINE_SIZE);
80	
81	/// A validated view over a pool region.
82	///
83	/// - Geometry is snapshotted out of the header at
84	///   init/attach: per-op paths never re-read fields a peer
85	///   could scribble on. Free-stack links live in shared
86	///   buffer memory and are validated at every pop instead.
87	pub struct Pool<'a> {
88	    /// The region's control block.
89	    header: &'a PoolHeader,
90	    /// Base of the buffer array
91	    /// (offset `size_of::<PoolHeader>()`).
92	    bufs: *mut u8,
93	    /// Snapshot of `header.buf_size`.
94	    buf_size: u32,
95	    /// Snapshot of `header.buf_count`.
96	    buf_count: u32,
97	    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,
98	}
99	
100	// SAFETY: the handle owns the allocator role; the shared
101	// state it touches (first_free_idx, next-links) is atomic,
102	// and buffer ownership is handed off with Release/Acquire
103	// ordering — same rationale as the ring endpoints.
104	unsafe impl Send for Pool<'_> {}
105	
106	impl<'a> Pool<'a> {

231	    pub fn alloc<T>(&mut self) -> Result<BufSlot<'a, T>, Exhausted>
232	    where
233	        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,
234	    {
235	        check_type::<T>(self.buf_size);
236	        loop {
237	            // Acquire pairs with free's Release CAS: seeing a
238	            // head index means seeing that buffer's next-link
239	            // store (and the freer's last writes) too.
240	            let head = self.header.first_free_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
241	            if head == NIL {
242	                return Err(Exhausted);
243	            }
244	            if head >= self.buf_count {
245	                return Err(Exhausted);
246	            }
247	            let next = self.next_buf_idx(head).load(Ordering::Relaxed);
248	            if next != NIL && next >= self.buf_count {
249	                return Err(Exhausted);
250	            }
251	            if self
252	                .header
253	                .first_free_idx
254	                .compare_exchange(head, next, Ordering::Acquire, Ordering::Relaxed)
255	                .is_ok()
256	            {
257	                return Ok(BufSlot {
258	                    header: self.header,
259	                    buf: self.buf_ptr(head),
260	                    idx: head,
261	                    _slot: PhantomData,
262	                });
263	            }
264	        }
265	    }
266	
267	    /// Buffer size in bytes (geometry snapshot).
268	    pub fn buf_size(&self) -> u32 {
269	        self.buf_size
270	    }
271	
272	    /// Buffer count (geometry snapshot).
273	    pub fn buf_count(&self) -> u32 {
274	        self.buf_count
275	    }
276	
277	    /// The next-free-buffer index cell of buffer `idx` — its
278	    /// first word, the intrusive free-stack link, meaningful
279	    /// only while the buffer is free.
280	    ///
281	    /// - Returns the atomic cell, not the value; symmetric
282	    ///   with the header's `first_free_idx`.
283	    /// - Callers pass `idx < buf_count` (validated pops /
284	    ///   init's linking loop), keeping the deref in bounds.
285	    fn next_buf_idx(&self, idx: u32) -> &AtomicU32 {
286	        let p = self.buf_ptr(idx) as *const AtomicU32;
287	        // SAFETY: idx < buf_count keeps the buffer in the
288	        // region validated at init/attach; the base is
289	        // cache-line aligned and buf_size is a line multiple,
290	        // so the first word is 4-aligned; all peers access it
291	        // as an atomic.
292	        unsafe { &*p }
293	    }
294	
295	    /// Pointer to buffer `idx`; callers pass
296	    /// `idx < buf_count`.
297	    fn buf_ptr(&self, idx: u32) -> *mut u8 {
298	        // SAFETY: idx < buf_count, so the offset stays inside
299	        // the buffer array validated at init/attach.
300	        unsafe { self.bufs.add(idx as usize * self.buf_size as usize) }
301	    }
302	}
303	
304	/// An allocated buffer, owned until [`free`](BufSlot::free):
305	/// `DerefMut` to use it as a `T` in place.

320	}
321	
322	/// Check `T` fits a slot; called once per `reserve_slot` (both
323	/// endpoints).
324	///
325	/// - Panics on a type-geometry mismatch — that is a programming
326	///   error, not a runtime condition.
327	fn check_type<T>(slot_size: u32) {
328	    assert!(size_of::<T>() <= slot_size as usize, "T larger than slot");
329	    assert!(
330	        core::mem::align_of::<T>() <= CACHE_LINE_SIZE,
331	        "T alignment exceeds slot alignment"
332	    );
333	}
334	
335	/// Pointer to the slot for free-running index `idx`.
336	///
337	/// - Returned range is `slot_size` bytes, cache-line aligned
338	///   (base is, and slot_size is a line multiple).
339	fn slot_ptr(slots: *mut u8, idx: u32, mask: u32, slot_size: u32) -> *mut u8 {
340	    let pos = (idx & mask) as usize;
341	    // SAFETY: pos < capacity, so the offset is inside the slot
342	    // array validated at init/attach.
343	    unsafe { slots.add(pos * slot_size as usize) }
344	}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/zc-ring-x1/src/pool.rs",
  "old_string": "/// An allocated buffer, owned until [`free`](BufSlot::free):\n/// `DerefMut` to use it as a `T` in place.",
  "new_string": "/// A non-allocating view over a pool: resolves validated\n/// buffer indices to owned [`BufSlot`] guards on behalf of a\n/// [`PoolRegistry`](crate::PoolRegistry).\n///\n/// - Created by [`Pool::resolver`]; carries the same header\n///   ref, buffer base, and geometry snapshot as its pool.\n/// - Cannot allocate — the single-popper token stays with the\n///   owning [`Pool`] handle.\n/// - Identifies its pool by header address (see\n///   [`PoolRegistry::into_desc`](crate::PoolRegistry::into_desc)).\n#[derive(Clone, Copy)]\npub struct PoolResolver<'a> {\n    /// The pool's control block.\n    header: &'a PoolHeader,\n    /// Base of the buffer array (copy of [`Pool::bufs`]).\n    bufs: *mut u8,\n    /// Snapshot of `header.buf_size`.\n    buf_size: u32,\n    /// Snapshot of `header.buf_count`.\n    buf_count: u32,\n}\n\n// SAFETY: the view is read-only over its own fields; the only\n// shared-memory mutation reachable through it is the minted\n// guards' free CAS, which is the free-stack's any-thread MPSC\n// push side. Allocation (the single-popper role) is not\n// reachable from a resolver.\nunsafe impl Send for PoolResolver<'_> {}\n// SAFETY: all methods take &self and touch shared state only\n// through atomics (as above), so concurrent use from multiple\n// threads adds no non-atomic shared access.\nunsafe impl Sync for PoolResolver<'_> {}\n\nimpl<'a> PoolResolver<'a> {\n    /// The pool's header address — its identity for registry\n    /// lookups.\n    pub(crate) fn header_ptr(&self) -> *const PoolHeader {\n        self.header\n    }\n\n    /// Buffer count (geometry snapshot).\n    pub(crate) fn buf_count(&self) -> u32 {\n        self.buf_count\n    }\n\n    /// Buffer size in bytes (geometry snapshot).\n    pub(crate) fn buf_size(&self) -> u32 {\n        self.buf_size\n    }\n\n    /// Mint the owned guard for buffer `idx`.\n    ///\n    /// # Safety\n    ///\n    /// - The caller has validated `idx < buf_count` and `T`'s\n    ///   geometry against this pool.\n    /// - The caller holds the buffer's ownership (the index\n    ///   came from a consumed guard via\n    ///   [`PoolRegistry::into_desc`](crate::PoolRegistry::into_desc),\n    ///   arrived with happens-before ordering, and is resolved\n    ///   exactly once) — minting a second live guard for one\n    ///   buffer aliases `&mut T`.\n    pub(crate) unsafe fn slot_from_idx<T>(&self, idx: u32) -> BufSlot<'a, T>\n    where\n        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,\n    {\n        // SAFETY: idx < buf_count (caller contract), so the\n        // offset stays inside the buffer array validated at\n        // init/attach.\n        let buf = unsafe { self.bufs.add(idx as usize * self.buf_size as usize) };\n        BufSlot {\n            header: self.header,\n            buf,\n            idx,\n            _slot: PhantomData,\n        }\n    }\n}\n\n/// An allocated buffer, owned until [`free`](BufSlot::free):\n/// `DerefMut` to use it as a `T` in place."
}
